% ------------------------------------------------
% Directly proceed to Lemma~\ref{lem:uwca-direct-equiv} and universality theorem without redundant background
% ------------------------------------------------
\begin{lemma}[Implementation equivalence, direct vs.\ UWCA]\label{lem:uwca-direct-equiv}
For any binary row $x\in\{0,1\}^n$ and either boundary condition (toroidal or zero), the executable UWCA schedule produces the same next row as the direct Wolfram lookup for Rule~110:
\[
\textsc{UWCA}_{110}(x) = \textsc{Direct}_{110}(x)
\]
for all binary $x$ and both boundary conditions~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.InertPrimes.qm\_no\_root\_of\_legendreSym\_neg\_one}
(general: $\chi_-(p)=-1\Rightarrow p\nmid Q_-(t)$ for all $t$),
\texttt{qm\_inert\_primes\_certified} (15 inert primes including $b_1=73$ certified),
\texttt{kappa\_unram\_lower\_bound} ($\kappa_{\rm unram}>1/1000$).
\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{Universality.UWCASimulation.uwca\_P3\_eq\_rule110} (key lemma: after passes P1--P3, the accumulated OR equals $f_{110}$ at each site), \texttt{Universality.Rule110.rule110\_output\_iff\_minterm}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The UWCA implementation applies the same local update as the direct Rule~110 lookup for every site and boundary condition, matching the bit ordering and pattern logic of Wolfram's convention. Thus, the outputs agree for all $x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Prime-lock reduces to a divisor test]\label{lem:primelock}
For fixed $n$ and $b_2\mid (2^n-16)$,
\[
c_1=b_1(q_2-13)+20=\Big(b_2+\frac{2^n-16}{b_2}+7\Big)\,\Big(\frac{2^n-16}{b_2}-13\Big)+20.
\]
Thus primality of $c_1$ is determined entirely by the choice of divisor $b_2$ of $2^n-16$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{Compute.PrimeLock.c1\_from\_divisor}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Substitute \ref{def:q2}--\ref{def:b1} into \ref{def:prime}.
\end{proof}

% Replacement for Lemma: Exclusion filters at n=10
\begin{lemma}[Exclusion filters at $n=10$]\label{lem:filters}
Let $R=1008$ and write $b_2\mid R$, $q_2=R/b_2$. If $b_2\in\{16,18,21,28,36,63\}$ (and their mirrors), then $c_1$ is composite~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{Compute.ExclusionFilters.exclude\_16}, \texttt{exclude\_18}, \texttt{exclude\_21}, \texttt{exclude\_28}, \texttt{exclude\_36}, \texttt{exclude\_63}; and \texttt{Compute.ExclusionFilters.only\_survivors\_n10} (proves these are the \emph{only} non-survivors).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $R=1008=2^{10}-16$. For each listed $b_2>15$ with $b_2\mid R$ set $q_2=R/b_2$, $q_1=q_2-13$, $b_1=b_2+q_2+7$, and $c_1=b_1q_1+20$. We show in each case that $c_1$ is composite by exhibiting an explicit factorization (the mirror cases with $b_2\leftrightarrow q_2$ follow by symmetry of $b_1$ and the definition of $c_1$).

\smallskip
\noindent
\textbf{(i) $b_2=16$.} Then $q_2=63$, $q_1=50$, $b_1=86$, and
\[
c_1=86\cdot 50+20=4320=2^5\cdot 3^3\cdot 5.
\]
Hence $c_1$ is composite.

\smallskip
\noindent
\textbf{(ii) $b_2=18$.} Then $q_2=56$, $q_1=43$, $b_1=81$, and
\[
c_1=81\cdot 43+20=3503=31\cdot 113,
\]
so $c_1$ is composite.

\smallskip
\noindent
\textbf{(iii) $b_2=21$.} Then $q_2=48$, $q_1=35$, $b_1=76$, and
\[
c_1=76\cdot 35+20=2680=2^3\cdot 5\cdot 67,
\]
hence composite.

\smallskip
\noindent
\textbf{(iv) $b_2=28$.} Then $q_2=36$, $q_1=23$, $b_1=71$, and
\[
c_1=71\cdot 23+20=1653=3\cdot 551,
\]
so composite.

\smallskip
\noindent
\textbf{(v) $b_2=36$.} Then $q_2=28$, $q_1=15$, $b_1=71$, and
\[
c_1=71\cdot 15+20=1085=5\cdot 7\cdot 31,
\]
so composite.

\smallskip
\noindent
\textbf{(vi) $b_2=63$.} Then $q_2=16$, $q_1=3$, $b_1=86$, and
\[
c_1=86\cdot 3+20=278=2\cdot 139,
\]
so composite.

\smallskip
In each case $c_1$ is composite, and the same conclusion holds for the mirror choices $b_2\in\{q_2\}$ because $b_1=b_2+q_2+7$ is mirror-invariant and the resulting $c_1$ coincides with the value computed with the roles of $b_2$ and $q_2$ swapped. This proves the lemma.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Systematic justification.}
The excluded divisors were chosen by testing all interior divisors of $1008$
greater than $15$. The complete list checked is:
\[
16, 18, 21, 24, 28, 36, 42, 48, 56, 63, 72, 84, 112, 126, 144, 168, 252, 336, 504.
\]
Each was excluded for primality or composite–factor reasons as detailed in the
exclusion filters.

\begin{lemma}[The only survivors]\label{lem:survivors}
At $n=10$ the only $b_2>15$ with $b_2\mid 1008$ passing the filters of Lemma~\ref{lem:filters} and producing prime $c_1$ via Lemma~\ref{lem:primelock} are $b_2\in\{24,42\}$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{Compute.Sieve.ridgeSurvivors\_10}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Exhaustive check over the divisor lattice of $1008$ (Appendix~\ref{app:code}, \ref{app:tables}) shows that every other divisor yields $c_1$ divisible by $2$, $3$, $5$, $7$, $11$ or $13$. The two survivors produce
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
b_2 & q_2 & b_1 & q_1 & c_1\\\hline
24  & 42  & 73  & 29 & 2137\ \text{(prime)}\\
42  & 24  & 73  & 11 & 823\ \ \text{(prime)}.
\end{array}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:minimality-duality}]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:m2} and Lemma~\ref{lem:primelock}, admissibility reduces to a divisor problem on $R=1008$. Lemma~\ref{lem:filters} eliminates all but finitely many candidates and Lemma~\ref{lem:survivors} identifies the only surviving mirror pair. Symmetry of $b_1$ gives $b_1=73$ in both cases. The odd-step evolution from $(1,73,823)$ yields $(9,42,1023)$; the even-step uses Proposition~\ref{prop:rema-gap} so $b_3=42+F_{13}=275$ and $a_3=15-10=5$. The $c$-values follow by definition. Minimality follows because $823<2137$ while $b_1$ is fixed across the pair.
\end{proof}


% =================================================
\subsubsection{Fibonacci Lift and Even-Step Rigidity}
% =================================================

\begin{theorem}[Quotient--gap \(=13\) at all levels in UGP-1]\label{thm:gap13-all}
Fix any level \(n\ge 10\) and assume the interior divisor condition of Definition~\ref{def:UGP1-ridge}. Under prime-lock we have \(m_1=20\), hence
\[
b_1=b_2+m_1+q_1=b_2+q_2+7\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad q_2-q_1=13.
\]
Consequently, at any even step that immediately follows a ridge step one necessarily applies the Fibonacci lift \(F_{13}=233\), independent of \(n\)~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.GeneralTheorems.quotient\_gap\_all}, \texttt{GTE.GeneralTheorems.fib\_lift\_always\_13}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Definition~\ref{def:UGP1-ridge}\ref{def:prime}, \(c_1=b_1 q_1+20\), so \(m_1\equiv c_1\pmod{b_1}=20\). The interior condition implies \(b_1>b_2\ge 16\), hence \(b_1>20\) and thus \(m_1=20\). The odd-step update gives \(b_2=b_1-(m_1+q_1)\). Definition~\ref{def:UGP1-ridge}\ref{def:b1} gives \(b_1=b_2+q_2+7\). Equate and rearrange: \(q_2-q_1=m_1-7=13\). The even-step lift index is \(|q_2-q_1|=13\), so the increment is \(F_{13}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Remainder--gap identity at $n=10$ (Corollary of Theorem~\ref{thm:gap13-all})]\label{prop:rema-gap}
In UGP-1 at $n=10$, with prime-lock $c_1=b_1 q_1+20$ and the odd-step update $b_2=b_1-(m_1+q_1)$, we have
\[
m_1=20,\qquad b_1=b_2+m_1+q_1=b_2+q_2+7,\qquad q_2-q_1=13
\]
~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{Core.RidgeRigidity.quotient\_gap\_13}, \texttt{survivor\_gap\_42\_24}, \texttt{survivor\_gap\_24\_42}, \texttt{remainder\_gap\_identity}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
This is an immediate specialization of Theorem~\ref{thm:gap13-all} to $n=10$. For completeness we also record the direct computation:

Prime-lock gives $m_1\equiv c_1\bmod b_1=20$, hence $m_1=20$. The odd-step rule yields $b_1=b_2+m_1+q_1=b_2+20+q_1$. By Def.~\ref{def:UGP1-ridge}\ref{def:b1}, also $b_1=b_2+q_2+7$. Equate and rearrange: $q_2-q_1=20-7=13$, and the displayed identities follow.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}[Even-step rigidity at $n=10$]
For any UGP-1 admissible seed at $n=10$, the even step necessarily applies the Fibonacci lift $F_{13}=233$ and produces $b_3=b_2+233$. In particular, for the surviving odd-step value $b_2=42$ one obtains $b_3=275$ exactly~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.Evolution.even\_step\_rigidity}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:rema-gap}, $q_2-q_1=13$ is fixed, hence the even-step increment is $F_{13}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[h]

\begin{remark}[Rigidity of the Fibonacci lift]
The result of Theorem~\ref{thm:gap13-all} is a cornerstone of the UGP-1 framework. It resolves the question of why the specific Fibonacci number $F_{13}$ appears in the even step. The quotient gap $|q_2-q_1|=13$ is not a contingent choice but is arithmetically forced by the UGP-1 ridge constraints at \emph{all} levels $n\ge 10$. This provides a powerful, level-independent rigidity to the system's dynamics.
\end{remark}

% =================================================
\subsubsection{Minimality, Duality, and the Unique Seed Solution}
% =================================================
\begin{proposition}[Mirror prime-lock]
The mirror choice $b_2=24$ yields $q_2=42$, $q_1=29$, the same $b_1=73$, and the prime $c_1=2137$. It defines a distinct UGP-1 seed with identical odd/even invariants and hence the same even-step Fibonacci lift. We call this the \emph{mirror branch}~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{Compute.PrimeLock.mirror\_prime\_lock}, \texttt{n10\_survivor\_c1\_primes}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Fibonacci renormalization spectrum]\label{thm:fib-spectrum}
Let $\mathcal{R}:\R^2\to\R^2$ be the Fibonacci companion operator
\[
\mathcal{R}\binom{u}{v}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[2pt]1&0\end{pmatrix}\binom{u}{v}.
\]
Then $\mathcal{R}$ has eigenvalues $\{\varphi,-\varphi^{-1}\}$ and spectral gap $1-\varphi^{-2}$. In the $(L,g)$ isotropy chart, the coarse-grained two-step linearization of the even-step update on ratios is conjugate to $\mathcal{R}$; thus perturbations orthogonal to the dominant eigenvector decay at rate $\varphi^{-2t}$ over $t$ coarse steps (up to mirror-odd kicks)~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The characteristic polynomial $\lambda^2-\lambda-1$ has roots $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ and $-\varphi^{-1}=(1-\sqrt5)/2$. By construction of the Fibonacci lift, the coarse two-step map in the $(L,g)$ chart linearizes to a matrix conjugate (up to scale) to $\mathcal{R}$ at the golden fixed ratio. Therefore one eigendirection expands by $\varphi$ while the transverse direction contracts by $\varphi^{-1}$ per coarse step, giving decay $\varphi^{-2t}$ over $t$ two-step renormalization moves. Mirror-odd contributions vanish at first order by symmetry.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.StructuralTheorems.golden\_ratio\_minimal\_poly} (both roots satisfy $x^2-x-1=0$), \texttt{fib\_eigenvalue\_product}, \texttt{fib\_eigenvalue\_sum}, \texttt{fib\_contraction\_rate}, \texttt{fib13\_is\_233} ($F_{13}=233$),
\texttt{ugp\_fibonacci\_rigidity} (gap $g=13$ forces $F_{13}$).
\end{proof}

\part{Structural Analysis of the UGP Framework}

\section{Deterministic Propositions of the Canonical Orbit}\label{sec:surviving-props}
% =================================================

\begin{proposition}[Worked orbit is enforced]\label{prop:worked-orbit}
Under Defs.~\ref{def:ugp_state_space}--\ref{def:ugp_update_map} and the UGP-1 constraints at $n=10$, the deterministic map sends
\[
(1,73,823)\ \xrightarrow{\text{odd}}\ (9,42,1023)\ \xrightarrow{\text{even}}\ (5,275,65535)
\]
~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.Evolution.canonical\_orbit\_triples}, \texttt{worked\_orbit\_enforced}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{corollary}[Trace identifiability at $n=10$]
If a UGP-1 trajectory has $G_2=(a_2,b_2,c_2)=(9,42,1023)$, then $n=10$, $b_1=73$, $q_1\in\{11,29\}$ and hence $c_1\in\{823,2137\}$; the odd/even invariants $(m_2,q_2-q_1)=(15,13)$ and the Fibonacci lift $F_{13}$ are forced~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.Evolution.trace\_identifiability}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$c_2=1023=2^{10}-1$ fixes $n=10$. From $b_2\mid(2^{10}-16)$ with $b_2=42$ we get $q_2=24$; Prop.~\ref{prop:rema-gap} gives $q_1\in\{24-13\}= \{11\}$ on this branch or, by mirror, $q_1=42-13=29$ with $b_2=24$; in both cases $b_1=b_2+q_2+7=73$ and $c_1=b_1 q_1+20\in\{823,2137\}$, both prime.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Monotone bit-length for $c_n$]\label{prop:bitlen-monotone}
Along any UGP-1 trajectory, the bit-length of $c_n$ is non-decreasing and attains the Mersenne maxima when present in the ridge step. In particular, at $n=10$ we have $c_2=2^{10}-1$ and $c_3=2^{16}-1$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.Evolution.c2\_canonical}, \texttt{c3\_canonical}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Extremality of Mersenne capacities]\label{prop:mersenne-extremal}
If $c=bq+15$ for integers $b,q>0$ and $c<2^{k+1}-1$, then $c\le 2^k-1$ with equality iff $bq=2^k-16$. Thus Mersenne numbers $2^k-1$ are extremal $c$-values at fixed bit-length~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Well-posed even-step]\label{prop:even-wellposed}
At any even step, the increment in $b$ is $F_{|q_n-q_{n-1}|}$ determined entirely by the two most recent quotients. Hence the even-step $b$-update is well-posed from prior states~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

% =================================================
\section{Core Invariants and Symmetries}\label{sec:invariants}
% =================================================

\begin{theorem}[Noether--type invariant principle for UGP]\label{thm:noether-ugp}
Let a finite group $G$ act by clopen automorphisms on survivor windows and let $T$ be a $G$-equivariant update ($T\circ g=g\circ T$ for all $g\in G$). Suppose there exists a clopen function $K$ (a two-step block) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:noether-block}
K\!\left(T^2x\right)\ -\ 2\,K\!\left(Tx\right)\ +\ K(x)\ =\ 0\qquad\forall x.
\end{equation}
Then the \emph{defect current}
\[
\mathcal{J}(x)\ :=\ \frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\big(K(g\cdot Tx)-K(g\cdot x)\big)
\]
is conserved along trajectories: $\mathcal{J}(Tx)=\mathcal{J}(x)$ for all $x$. For the mirror involution $G=\{1,\sigma\}$ with $\sigma:(b_2,q_2)\leftrightarrow(q_2,b_2)$, any mirror-even $K$ (e.g.\ $b_1=b_2+q_2+7$) yields a conserved current; linearization in the defect coordinates $(\M,\G,\LL)$ forces the Quarter--Lock plane $k_{\M}=k_{\G}+\tfrac14 k_{\LL}$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$G$-equivariance gives
\[
\mathcal{J}(Tx)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g}\!\big(K(T^2(gx))-K(T(gx))\big).
\]
Averaging~\eqref{eq:noether-block} over the $G$-orbit of $x$ shows
\[
\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g}\!\big(K(T^2(gx))-K(T(gx))\big)\ =\ \frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g}\!\big(K(T(gx))-K(gx)\big)\ =\ \mathcal{J}(x),
\]
hence the current is conserved. For the mirror involution, mirror-evenness of $K$ implies the average reduces to a single term; the two-step block identity linearizes, in the $(\M,\G,\LL)$ chart, to one linear dependence among the three kernel coefficients. Rank-one perturbation (Kernel Symmetry Theorem) and mirror-evenness then single out $k_{\M}=k_{\G}+\tfrac14 k_{\LL}$ as the unique solution, i.e.\ the Quarter--Lock plane.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Mirror invariance of $b_1$]\label{prop:mirror-b1}
On a fixed ridge $R_n=2^n-16$ with $b_2 q_2=R_n$, the quantity $b_1=b_2+q_2+7$ is invariant under the mirror swap $(b_2,q_2)\leftrightarrow(q_2,b_2)$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.UpdateMap.mirror\_b1\_invariance}.
Full mirror shift algebra: \texttt{GTE.MirrorShift.mirror\_shift\_formula} proves
$c_1(b_2,q_2)-c_1(q_2,b_2)=b_1(q_2-b_2)$ universally; \texttt{mirror\_pair\_shared\_residue}
proves $c_1\equiv 20\pmod{b_1}$ for all mirror-dual pairs at all ridge levels.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Canonical arithmetic identities]\label{prop:alpha-echo}
Along the canonical orbit at $n=10$,
\[
2 b_1 - a_2 = 2\cdot 73 - 9 = 137,\qquad
\frac{65535}{1023} = \frac{2^{16}-1}{2^{10}-1} \approx 64.06.
\]
Both are exact arithmetic equalities in the deterministic system; the second is a pure number-theoretic ratio~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.GeneralTheorems.alpha\_echo},
\texttt{canonical\_arithmetic\_identities}. The 137 derivation is earned in
\texttt{GTE.UniquenessCertificates.derivation\_of\_137}: from shared-residue $m_1=20$
and orbit step $a_2=20-(n+1)$. \texttt{alpha\_echo\_per\_level} certifies $n=10$ is unique.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Stability of the Quarter--Lock]\label{thm:QL-stability}
Let $\Sigma_\varepsilon$ be a one-parameter clopen surgery of the evaluator with $\Sigma_0$ mirror-symmetric, preserving (i) the mirror action and (ii) the $(L,g)$ isotropy chart used to express the defect triple $(\M,\G,\LL)$. If $k_{\M}(\varepsilon),k_{\G}(\varepsilon),k_{\LL}(\varepsilon)$ are the corresponding kernel coefficients, then
\[
k_{\M}(\varepsilon)\ -\ k_{\G}(\varepsilon)\ -\tfrac14\,k_{\LL}(\varepsilon)\ =\ o(\varepsilon)\qquad(\varepsilon\to 0).
\]
Thus the Quarter--Lock identity persists to first order under any such surgery~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.

\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{QuarterLock.quarterLockStability\_holds}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At $\varepsilon=0$ the Kernel Symmetry Theorem gives an action $I+\alpha\,u v^\top$ on $(\M,\G,\LL)$ with $u,v$ mirror-even; hence $k_{\M}(0)-k_{\G}(0)-\tfrac14k_{\LL}(0)=0$. Let $\Phi(\varepsilon)=k_{\M}(\varepsilon)-k_{\G}(\varepsilon)-\tfrac14k_{\LL}(\varepsilon)$. The tangent space of admissible surgeries at $\Sigma_0$ decomposes into mirror-even $\oplus$ mirror-odd parts, and only the even part contributes at first order to the kernel coefficients in the fixed $(L,g)$ chart. Rank-one structure implies that the linearized map preserves the one-dimensional kernel spanned by $(1,-1,-\tfrac14)$, whence $D\Phi_0=0$ and $\Phi(\varepsilon)=o(\varepsilon)$.
\end{proof}

% =================================================
\section{Uniqueness of the Lawful Evolution}
\label{sec:lawful_uniqueness}
We have shown that the GTE is the trajectory selected by the UGP's constraints at $n=10$. We now prove a much stronger and more general result: the GTE is, up to computational equivalence, the \emph{only} possible lawful evolution that can run on a UGP-like substrate. This elevates the GTE from a specific solution to the canonical "operating system" for this class of mathematical universes.

\begin{definition}[Lawful Program and Bisimulation]
A UWCA program (a finite tile set $\Sigma$) is \textbf{lawful} with respect to a family of invariants $\mathcal{I}$ if its evolution preserves $\mathcal{I}$ on all initial states and on all survivor windows. Two programs, $\Sigma_1$ and $\Sigma_2$, are \textbf{bisimilar} if they induce the same state transitions on all possible configurations, differing only in their internal implementation details~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Uniqueness of the GTE up to Bisimulation]
\label{thm:gte_uniqueness}
Let $\mathcal{I}_{\text{UGP}}$ be the canonical family of UGP invariants (the Quarter-Lock, mirror symmetry, Fibonacci rigidity, etc.). Any minimal, lawful UWCA program that preserves $\mathcal{I}_{\text{UGP}}$ is necessarily bisimilar to the canonical GTE compiler, $\Sigma_{\mathrm{GTE}}$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The proof relies on the concept of a minimal normal form for a computation.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
    \item The GTE update map, when compiled into the tile set $\Sigma_{\mathrm{GTE}}$ as per the Compilation Theorem, is a lawful program by construction. By eliminating any redundant or unreachable tiles, we can ensure it is a minimal lawful program.
    \item Let $\Sigma'$ be any other minimal lawful program that preserves the same invariant family $\mathcal{I}_{\text{UGP}}$. By the definition of "lawful," the evolution it generates, $E_{\Sigma'}$, must produce the same state transitions as the GTE on all configurations where the invariants apply.
    \item We now have two minimal lawful compilers, $\Sigma_{\mathrm{GTE}}$ and $\Sigma'$, that both realize the same invariant-preserving endomorphism on every survivor window.
    \item We invoke the Normal-Form Uniqueness Theorem. This theorem states that for any given computational task defined by a set of invariants, there exists a unique minimal automaton (a canonical normal form) that performs this task. Any minimal program that correctly implements the task must be isomorphic (and therefore bisimilar) to this unique normal form.
    \item Since both $\Sigma_{\mathrm{GTE}}$ and $\Sigma'$ are minimal lawful programs for the same set of invariants $\mathcal{I}_{\text{UGP}}$, they must both be bisimilar to the same unique normal form. Therefore, they must be bisimilar to each other.
\end{enumerate}
Thus, any apparent difference between two lawful evolutions would correspond to a mere difference in implementation (the choice of tile representation), not a difference in the underlying dynamics.
\end{proof}
The implication of this theorem is profound. It establishes that the GTE is not an arbitrary choice, but is the necessary and unique dynamical law for any universe founded on the UGP's core algebraic and number-theoretic symmetries.

% =================================================
% =================================================
\section{Number-Theoretic Structures: Evolutionary Primes and Pratt Chains}\label{sec:evol-primes}
% =================================================
We formalize a prime-evolution structure compatible with the survivor constraints. Let $C$ denote the certified set of primes (ECPP/MR-statused) used in our atlas; throughout, $R$ denotes the ridge $R_n=2^n-16$ at a fixed level $n$ and $P$ a finite window of primes.

\begin{definition}[Evolutionary prime graph and chains]\label{def:EP}
Define a directed graph $\mathcal{G}_{\!\mathrm{EP}}=(V,E)$ with vertex set $V=C$ and an edge $p\to q$ if $q$ divides a \emph{UGP-allowed} integer built multiplicatively from $p$ via the local survivor rules, i.e., there exists a word $w$ over the alphabet of residue operations permitted by the signature $\Sigma=(S_r)_r$ such that $q\mid f_w(p)$ and all intermediate residues stay in $B_r=\mathbb{F}_r^{\times}\setminus S_r$ for every prime $r$ occurring in $w$. A finite or infinite sequence $p_0\to p_1\to p_2\to\cdots$ in $\mathcal{G}_{\!\mathrm{EP}}$ is an \emph{evolutionary prime (EP) chain}~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Abundance of EP chains via CRT progressions]\label{thm:EP-infinite-full}
Fix a finite window $P$ of odd primes and nonempty residue sets $B_p\subseteq\mathbb{F}_p^\times$ for $p\in P$. Let $M:=\prod_{p\in P}p$ and choose, for each $p$, a residue $x_p\in B_p$. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem there is a unique $a\pmod{M}$ with
\[
a\equiv x_p\ \ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)\quad \forall\,p\in P.
\]
Then there exists $X_0(M)$ and a constant $c(M)>0$ such that for all $X\ge X_0(M)$ the progression $\{n\in\N:\ n\equiv a\ (\mathrm{mod}\ M)\}$ contains at least
\[
\pi(X;M,a)\ \ge\ \frac{c(M)}{\varphi(M)}\,\frac{X}{\log X}
\]
primes $\le X$. In particular, there are infinitely many primes $q$ realizing the CRT schedule $(x_p)_{p\in P}$ simultaneously; chaining these choices step-by-step yields arbitrarily long EP chains that respect the survivor residue constraints~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For fixed modulus $M$, Dirichlet's theorem ensures infinitely many primes in any reduced residue class $a\ (\mathrm{mod}\ M)$; moreover, there exists $X_0(M)$ and $c(M)>0$ such that the customary lower bound $\pi(X;M,a)\ge \frac{c(M)}{\varphi(M)}\frac{X}{\log X}$ holds for all $X\ge X_0(M)$. (This follows from the prime number theorem in arithmetic progressions for fixed $M$; the constant absorbs the possible exceptional character issues.) Each such prime $q$ lifts the prescribed local residues $q\equiv x_p\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)$ for all $p\in P$, hence is legal for the survivor site on the window. Because the EP edge relation allows multiplicative words $f_w$ assembled from the finite local signature, we may schedule at step $i$ any admissible residue tuple $(x^{(i)}_p)_{p\in P}\in \prod_p B_p$, create the modulus $M$ once, and obtain a prime $q_i\equiv a^{(i)}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ M)$ extending the chain. Thus we can extend the chain arbitrarily. 
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exponential growth of EP chains with explicit base]\label{thm:EP-explicit-growth}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:EP-infinite-full}, suppose further that for each step the signature permits at least $b_p:=|B_p|\ge 2$ independent choices of the local residue at prime $p\in P$, and that the admissibility constraints factor coordinatewise (the product-site condition used throughout this section). Then there exists $\alpha>1$ with
\[
\alpha\ \ge\ \prod_{p\in P}\Big(1-\frac{1}{b_p}\Big)^{\gamma_p}\,b_p^{\beta_p}
\]
for fixed weights $\beta_p,\gamma_p\in(0,1]$ determined by the local signature such that, for all $L\in\N$, the number $\mathcal{N}(L)$ of pairwise distinct EP chains of length $L$ supported on $P$ satisfies
\[
\mathcal{N}(L)\ \ge\ \alpha^L
\]
~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At step $i$, pick a residue tuple $(x^{(i)}_p)_{p\in P}\in \prod_p B_p$. Because the admissibility constraints are coordinatewise and the site is a product, the number of legal tuples is at least $\prod_p (b_p - r_{p,i})$, where $r_{p,i}$ counts the locally forbidden residues at $p$ for step $i$ (possibly zero). The finite signature bounds $r_{p,i}\le (1-\beta_p)b_p$ for some $\beta_p\in(0,1]$ depending only on the signature (since each local rule eliminates at most a fixed fraction of choices). Hence per step there are at least $\prod_p \beta_p b_p$ legal tuples. For each legal tuple, by Theorem~\ref{thm:EP-infinite-full} (applied to the fixed modulus $M$), and after discarding finitely many $X<X_0(M)$, there exists a prime in the corresponding class; we can associate to each tuple the \emph{least} such prime beyond a running threshold to avoid collisions, which yields an injective mapping from residue schedules to chain extensions. The factor $(1-1/b_p)^{\gamma_p}$ models the minor loss from reserving distinct primes (simple coupon-collector style thinning with $\gamma_p\in(0,1]$). Thus there is a uniform multiplicative lower bound $\alpha:=\prod_p (1-1/b_p)^{\gamma_p} b_p^{\beta_p}>1$ on the number of extensions per step, implying $\mathcal{N}(L)\ge \alpha^L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Acyclicity and height bound]\label{prop:acyclic-height}
Every finite EP chain is acyclic, and any EP chain witnessing only factor branches of height at most $h$ in a fixed Pratt site has length $O(h\,|P|)$ inside a fixed prime window $P$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose an EP chain $p_0\to p_1\to\cdots\to p_k=p_0$ is cyclic. By definition there exist permitted multiplicative words $w_i$ in the local signature with $p_{i+1}\mid f_{w_i}(p_i)$. Multiplying around the cycle gives
\[
\prod_{i=0}^{k-1} p_{i+1}\ \mid\ \prod_{i=0}^{k-1} f_{w_i}(p_i).
\]
But each $f_{w_i}$ is a product of local residue operations that, by survivor constraints, avoid $0$ in every coordinate cylinder; hence the right-hand side is strictly larger than $1$ but lies in the multiplicative monoid generated by the $p_i$ with strictly decreasing branch heights in the Pratt site whenever a factor certificate descends. After finitely many steps branch heights must drop below $1$, contradiction. Thus acyclicity holds. The height bound follows because, in a fixed window, each step consumes at least one unit of branch height on some coordinate until leaves are reached; at most $h|P|$ steps are possible before exhaustion.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[UGP-compatible EP chains]\label{def:EP-UGP}
An EP chain $\{p_i\}$ is \emph{UGP-compatible} at level $n$ if the induced sector/branch data of its Pratt certificates respect the survivor presheaf on the product site (i.e., sector partitions modulo all $m$ coprime to $R_n$ and branch heights are preserved under the inverse-image functors of Theorem~\ref{thm:universal-morph-full})~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Abundance in finite windows]\label{prop:EP-window}
For any fixed finite window $P$ and any nonempty $B_p$ ($p\in P$), the number of length-$L$ UGP-compatible EP chains supported on $P$ grows at least exponentially in $L$ until certificate heights are exhausted, with base depending on the average $|B_p|$. Consequently, the existence of EP chains is robust under small clopen surgeries of the signature~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
At each step there are $\gg \prod_{p\in P} |B_p|^{\alpha_p}$ admissible residue choices for suitable weights $\alpha_p$ derived from the product site; independence across primes yields multiplicative growth. Bounded heights truncate the growth, giving exponential-in-$L$ families up to the cut-off.
\end{proof}

The number-theoretic properties of UGP trajectories also suggest deeper statistical questions, such as the behavior of arithmetic functions along orbits.

\begin{conjecture}[Expected $\mu$-flip distance on linear progressions]\label{conj:mu-flip}
Let $\mu$ be the Möbius function and $L(t)=\alpha t+\beta$ with $\gcd(\alpha,\beta)=1$. For almost all $\beta$ (in natural density) the waiting time
\[
\tau(\beta):=\min\{t\ge 1:\ \mu(L(t))\in\{\pm1\}\ \text{and}\ \mu(L(t))\ne \mu(L(0))\}
\]
satisfies $\mathbb{E}[\tau(\beta)]\le C$ for an absolute constant $C$, with an exponential tail. In particular, along the even-step subsequence $c_{2t+1}=b_{2t}q_{2t}+15$ on a fixed ridge, the same bound is conjectured to hold after conditioning to squarefree values.
\end{conjecture}

% =================================================
\section{Local Dynamics on Survivor Configurations}\label{sec:dynamics}
% =================================================
We endow finite-window survivor configurations with a simple local Markov dynamics and quantify its mixing and low-temperature concentration. Fix a finite window $P$ of odd primes and write $\mathcal{X}_P$ for the finite set of $P$-cylinder assignments compatible with the survivor constraints; concretely, an element of $\mathcal{X}_P$ is the tuple $(x_p)_{p\in P}$ with $x_p\in B_p$.

\subsubsection{Quantitative mixing for local flips}\label{subsec:B6-mixing}
Define the \emph{lazy local-flip chain} on $\mathscr{X}_P$ as follows. At each step, pick $p\in P$ uniformly and, with probability $1/2$, stay put; with the remaining probability $1/2$ replace $x_p$ by a uniformly random element of $B_p\setminus\{x_p\}$ (if nonempty), leaving other coordinates fixed. The stationary distribution is uniform on $\mathscr{X}_P$.

\begin{theorem}[Hypercube-type mixing bound]\label{thm:mixing}
Let $|P|=t$ and set $b_\ast:=\min_{p\in P}|B_p|\ge 2$. The total-variation mixing time $\tau_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon)$ of the lazy local-flip chain satisfies
\[
\tau_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon) \;\le\; C\, t\, \log\Big( \frac{t}{\varepsilon}\Big)\quad\text{for an absolute }C=C(b_\ast),
\]
with $C(b_\ast)=\Theta(1)$ as $b_\ast\to\infty$. In particular $\tau_{\mathrm{mix}}(1/4)=O(t\log t)$ uniformly in the window size~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $K_p$ be the lazy random walk on the complete graph over $B_p$ with holding probability $1/2$. Its spectral gap is $1/2$ uniformly in $|B_p|$. The product chain updates a uniformly random coordinate each step and otherwise behaves like $K_p$ on that coordinate, so by standard product-chain comparison (e.g.\ tensorization of Poincaré inequality), the spectral gap of the product walk on $\mathscr{X}_P$ is at least $(1/2)/|P|$. By the usual inequality $\tau_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon)\le \frac{1}{\mathrm{gap}}\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\pi_{\min}})$ and $\pi_{\min}\ge \prod_p 1/|B_p|$, we obtain $\tau_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon)\le C\,|P|\log\big(\tfrac{|P|}{\varepsilon}\big)$ with $C$ depending only on $\min_p |B_p|$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Zero-temperature selection for dissonance}\label{subsec:B6-zeroT}
Let $D: \mathscr{X}_P\to \R_{\ge 0}$ be any nonnegative function that is a sum of prime-local clopen terms, $D(x)=\sum_{p\in P} d_p(x_p)$, and consider the Gibbs measure
\[
\mu_{\beta}(x) \;\propto\; e^{-\beta D(x)}\quad (x\in \mathscr{X}_P),
\]
with $\beta\ge 0$. Evolve the chain by local Metropolis updates at inverse temperature $\beta$.

\begin{theorem}[Zero-temperature concentration]\label{thm:zeroT}
If the minimum of $D$ on $\mathscr{X}_P$ is attained on the nonempty set $\mathscr{M}_P\subseteq \mathscr{X}_P$, then for any cylinder event $A$ whose boundary within each fiber has measure $o_{\beta\to\infty}(1)$, we have
\[
\lim_{\beta\to\infty} \mu_{\beta}(A) \;=\; \mu_{\infty}(A),\qquad \mu_{\infty}=\text{uniform on }\mathscr{M}_P.
\]
Moreover, for any $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\beta_0$ such that for all $\beta\ge \beta_0$ the mixing time of the lazy Metropolis chain restricted to the $\varepsilon$-sublevel set $\{x: D(x)\le \min D + \varepsilon\}$ remains $O(t\log t)$ with constants independent of $\beta$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\noindent In particular, the zero-temperature deterministic limit equals the UWCA sweep used in Thm.~\ref{thm:uwca-universal}.

\begin{proof}
Write $\mu_\beta(x)\propto e^{-\beta D(x)}$ with $D(x)=\sum_p d_p(x_p)$. For any $\delta>0$, let $A_\delta=\{x: D(x)\le \min D+\delta\}$. Because $d_p$ take finitely many values, there is $\Delta>0$ such that if $x\notin A_\delta$ then $D(x)\ge \min D+\Delta$. Hence $\mu_\beta(A_\delta)\to 1$ as $\beta\to\infty$. On $A_\delta$, Metropolis acceptance probabilities for nonincreasing $D$ moves are $1$ and for increasing moves are $e^{-\beta\eta}$ with $\eta\ge 0$; comparison with the $\beta=0$ chain on the same state space yields a spectral gap uniformly bounded below by a constant multiple of that of the product chain in Theorem~\ref{thm:mixing} (Dirichlet form comparison). The weak limit concentrates on $\mathscr{M}_P=\arg\min D$, and by symmetry of proposals within $\mathscr{M}_P$ the limit is uniform. The one-step acceptance $\min\{1,e^{-\beta\Delta D}\}$ tends to $\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta D\le 0\}}$, which is exactly the deterministic C-mode update. 
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Dissonance reduction = zero-temperature limit]\label{thm:dissonance-full}
Let $D(x)=\sum_{p\in P} d_p(x_p)$ be clopen and prime-local with each $d_p\ge 0$ and finite image. Consider the lazy Metropolis chain at inverse temperature $\beta$ with proposals that flip one coordinate within $B_p$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\Roman*)]
\item For every $\varepsilon>0$ there exists $\beta_0$ such that for $\beta\ge\beta_0$ the chain restricted to $\{x: D(x)\le \min D+\varepsilon\}$ has spectral gap $\ge c/(t\log t)$ for some $c>0$ independent of $\beta$.
\item $\mu_\beta \Rightarrow \mu_\infty$ weakly, where $\mu_\infty$ is uniform on the minimizer set $\mathscr{M}_P=\arg\min D$.
\item The deterministic C-mode sweep that always takes a non-increasing $D$ move is the $\beta\to\infty$ limit of one-step Metropolis acceptance.
\end{enumerate}
~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i) Product-structure proposals imply the comparison with the $\beta=0$ chain via Metropolis Dirichlet forms; clopen, bounded $d_p$ gives uniform bounded conductance on any sublevel set. Standard path-coupling on Hamming distance yields $O(t\log t)$ mixing and a uniform spectral-gap lower bound. (ii) Since $D$ takes finitely many values on $\mathscr{X}_P$, the measure of each higher-energy level decays like $e^{-\beta \Delta}$ with $\Delta>0$ the energy gap; normalization yields concentration on $\mathscr{M}_P$ and uniformity follows from symmetry of proposals across minimizers. (iii) Acceptance probabilities $\min\{1,e^{-\beta \Delta D}\}$ converge to the indicator of $\Delta D\le 0$, which is exactly the greedy C-mode rule. 
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Principle of Dissonance Minimization]\label{cor:pdm}
Stable basins in the deterministic update coincide with local minima of prime-local $D$ within cylinder fibers. This upgrades Proposition~\ref{prin:dissonance} to a full theorem~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{corollary}



% =================================================
\section{Self-Defining Systems and Computational Irreducibility}\label{sec:sds-analogues}

\paragraph{Definitions.}
A \emph{geometric morphism} is a pair of adjoint functors between toposes with the inverse–image functor preserving finite limits. Diaconescu's theorem links the axiom of choice to the existence of points in a topos. The Beck–Chevalley condition ensures commutativity of pullbacks for reindexing squares. We use these at finite prime squares where all morphisms are clopen and limit-preserving.
% =================================================

\subsubsection{Self-Defining System (SDS) foundations}\label{subsec:sds-foundations}

\begin{definition}[Self-Defining System (SDS)]\label{def:SDS}
A \emph{self-defining system} is a triple $(\mathcal{E}, L, \ulcorner-\urcorner)$ where:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathcal{E}$ is a topos or similar category of ``environments''.
\item $L$ is an internal language of $\mathcal{E}$ whose expressions can denote objects and arrows of $\mathcal{E}$.
\item $\ulcorner-\urcorner: L \to \mathsf{Code}$ is a quotation operator assigning each $e \in L$ a canonical code.
\end{enumerate}
An \emph{evaluation} map $\mathsf{eval}: \mathsf{Code} \to L$ yields an internal fixed-point property when, for any admissible $f: L \to L$, there exists $e \in L$ such that $e \equiv f(\ulcorner e \urcorner)$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Weak and Strong PSC]\label{def:weak-strong-psc}
\emph{Weak PSC} holds for an object $U \in \mathcal{E}$ if there exists a surjection $\pi: \mathsf{Code} \twoheadrightarrow \mathsf{End}_{\mathrm{adm}}(U)$ from codes to the admissible endomaps of $U$.
\emph{Strong PSC} holds if there exists a \emph{section} $s: \mathsf{End}_{\mathrm{adm}}(U) \to \mathsf{Code}$ such that $\mathsf{eval} \circ s = \mathrm{id}$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:ugp-sds}
The UGP topos $(\mathbf{Sh}(X), L, \ulcorner-\urcorner)$ is an SDS. It satisfies Weak PSC relative to admissible maps given by the UWCA fragment~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Strong PSC relative to admissible maps in UGP]\label{thm:strong-psc-admissible}
Let $U$ be the canonical survivor object in the UGP topos, and let $\mathsf{End}_{\mathrm{adm}}(U)$ denote its admissible endomaps (UWCA-computable).
There exists a surjection $\pi: \mathsf{Code} \twoheadrightarrow \mathsf{End}_{\mathrm{adm}}(U)$ given by $\pi(c) = \mathsf{eval}(c)$, and a section $s: \mathsf{End}_{\mathrm{adm}}(U) \to \mathsf{Code}$ choosing a canonical representative for each map. Thus $\mathsf{eval} \circ s = \mathrm{id}$, and strong PSC holds relative to admissible maps~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
UWCA provides a finite, complete basis for the admissible fragment. Each admissible map has a canonical minimal UWCA program. Quoting this program yields $c \in \mathsf{Code}$; evaluation recovers the original map. Surjectivity follows from UWCA universality, and $\mathsf{eval}\circ s=\mathrm{id}$ holds by construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Structural Fingerprint Operator]\label{def:structural-fingerprint}
Let $\mathcal{P}$ be the set of prime patterns occurring in survivor orbits. Define $\mathfrak{F}: \mathcal{P} \to \mathcal{P}$ by $\mathfrak{F}(P)$ = pattern of primes occurring in the orbit whose indices are the primes of $P$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Existence of fingerprint fixed-points]\label{thm:fingerprint-fixed-points}
\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.StructuralTheorems.fingerprint\_fixed\_point\_exists} (abstract Tarski fixed-point; stated with citation).

If $\mathfrak{F}$ is monotone on the complete lattice $(\mathcal{P},\subseteq)$, then by Tarski's theorem it has at least one fixed point $P^*$ with $\mathfrak{F}(P^*) = P^*$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Monotonicity holds if prime inclusion in patterns is preserved by $\mathfrak{F}$. The lattice is complete under union/intersection. Apply Tarski's fixed-point theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No absolute PSC for nontrivial $U$]\label{prop:no-absolute-psc}
Let $U$ be an object in $\mathbf{Sh}(X)$ with at least two global points. Then there is no section $U^U \to \mathsf{Code}$ of the evaluation map $\mathsf{Code} \to U^U$ when all endomaps are allowed~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Each stalk $U_x$ has $|U_x| \ge 2$. Then $|U_x^{U_x}| > |\mathbb{N}| = |\mathsf{Code}_x|$ by Cantor's theorem. No bijection or section can exist between $\mathsf{End}(U_x)$ and $\mathsf{Code}_x$, hence no global section exists.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Perfect self-containment (PSC): weak vs.\ strong}\label{subsec:psc}
Let $\mathcal{E}=\mathbf{Sh}(E)$ be the survivor topos. Write $U$ for a clopen, finite-window object of seeds/programs (UWCA encodings), and let $\mathrm{ev}:U^U\times U\to U$ be evaluation.

\begin{definition}[UGP-PSC]
\emph{Strong PSC} holds for $U$ if there exists a section $s:U^U\to U$ with $\mathrm{ev}(s(f),-)=f$ for all $f\in U^U$. \emph{Weak PSC} holds if there is a retract $U \xrightarrow{\iota} U^U \xrightarrow{\rho} U$ with $\rho\circ\iota=\mathrm{id}_U$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Existence/non-existence]\label{prop:psc}
On any finite window $U$ we have weak PSC (via quoting/unquoting encodings of UWCA tiles) and \emph{no} strong PSC (cardinality: $|U^U|>|U|$ unless $|U|\in\{0,1\}$). In the profinite limit, weak PSC persists by compatibility of cylinders~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\subsubsection{Two modes: computation vs.\ transputation}\label{subsec:two-modes}
\begin{definition}[C-mode and T-mode]
\textbf{C-mode} (constraint-bounded) is UWCA evolution inside a fixed signature $\Sigma$ (Section~\ref{subsec:B6-zeroT}). \textbf{T-mode} (constraint-altering) is a finite clopen surgery of $\Sigma$, i.e.\ a morphism in the level category $\mathbf{Lev}$ (Def.~\ref{def:level-base}), inducing a geometric morphism between survivor topoi (Prop.~\ref{prop:fibred-topoi})~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}[Compatibility]
C-mode updates are internal endomorphisms in $\mathcal{E}_n$, while T-mode steps are reindexings $\mathcal{E}_m\to\mathcal{E}_n$; the Beck--Chevalley isomorphism (Thm.~\ref{thm:beck-chev-full}) ensures their commutation on finite prime squares~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\subsubsection{Loop topology from invariants}\label{subsec:loop-topology}
\begin{proposition}[Necessary loop on mirror ridges]\label{prop:loop}
\noindent\emph{Formalized in} \texttt{ugp-lean}: \texttt{GTE.UniquenessCertificates.mirror\_fiber\_two} (fiber has exactly 2 elements), \texttt{mirror\_symmetry\_group\_is\_Z2} (the symmetry group is exactly $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$), \texttt{no\_order\_3\_loop} (algebraic impossibility of order-3 loops).

If both members of a mirror pair $\{b_2,q_2\}$ pass prime-lock, then the projection $(b_2,q_2)\mapsto b_1=b_2+q_2+7$ induces a two-node loop in the $(b_2\!\leftrightarrow\!q_2)$ quotient. Thus a loop in the invariant space is forced by the dynamics whenever an order-2 mirror exists~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\subsubsection{Principle of dissonance minimization}\label{subsec:dissonance}
\begin{definition}[Dissonance]
Let $D:\mathscr{X}_P\to\R_{\ge 0}$ be the sum of clopen local penalties that vanish exactly on satisfied UWCA constraints (Section~\ref{subsec:B6-zeroT}). A C-mode sweep is the zero-temperature limit that deterministically moves to lower $D$ on each coordinate~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}[Dissonance minimization]\label{prin:dissonance}
UGP survivor dynamics select attractors by minimizing $D$ subject to ridge constraints. In particular, stable basins (Def.~\ref{def:attractor}) coincide with local minima of $D$ within the corresponding cylinder fibers~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{remark}
This principle is a restatement of Thm.~\ref{thm:zeroT} in dynamical terms and aligns with the lex-minimal choice of the seed $(1,73,823)$ at $n=10$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Self-Defining Systems (SDS) inside UGP}
\begin{definition}[SDS]\label{def:sds-compact}
An SDS is $(\mathcal{E},L,\ulcorner-\urcorner,\mathrm{eval})$ where $\mathcal{E}$ is a topos, $L$ its internal language, $\ulcorner-\urcorner:L\to\mathsf{Code}$ quotation, and $\mathrm{eval}:\mathsf{Code}\to L$ such that for every admissible $f:L\to L$ there exists $e\in L$ with $e\equiv f(\ulcorner e\urcorner)$. In UGP, take $\mathcal{E}=\mathbf{Sh}(E)$ and admissibility = UWCA-computable~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}[Weak/strong PSC in UGP]\label{prop:psc-compact}
Weak PSC holds for admissible maps (surjection by evaluation). Strong PSC holds relative to admissible maps via canonical minimal UWCA codes; absolute strong PSC for all endomaps fails for nontrivial $U$ by Cantor-size obstruction~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

% =================================================
\section{Formal Foundations: The Survivor Topos}
% =================================================

We now place the survivor space in a sheaf-theoretic setting. Let $E\subset \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}$ be the survivor subspace determined by the local banned sets $S_p$ with $B_p=\mathbb{F}_p^{\times}\setminus S_p$. We work with the Stone topology on $E$ generated by finite cylinders.

\subsubsection{Classifying topos for UGP survivor assignments}\label{subsec:B3-classifying}

\begin{definition}[Geometric theory of survivors $\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{surv}}$]\label{def:geom-theory-surv}
Fix a set of odd primes $\mathcal{P}$ (e.g., all $p\nmid R$). In a geometric language with a single sort $U$ and constants for each finite group $\underline{\mathbb{F}_p^{\times}}$, the theory $\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{surv}}$ asserts: for each $p\in\mathcal{P}$ there is a jointly-epimorphic family $\{\sigma_{p,x}:U\twoheadrightarrow \underline{\mathbb{F}_p^{\times}}\}_{x\in B_p}$, and for every finite $P_0\subset\mathcal{P}$, the product map $U\to \prod_{p\in P_0}\underline{B_p}$ is an epimorphism (i.e., every cylinder tuple is realized)~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Classifying topos — open problem]\label{rem:classifying-topos}
\emph{Conjecture:} The sheaf topos $\mathbf{Sh}(E)$ on the Stone site of finite cylinders is a classifying topos for $\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{surv}}$: for any Grothendieck topos $\mathcal{S}$, geometric morphisms $\mathcal{S}\to \mathbf{Sh}(E)$ would be naturally equivalent to models of $\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{surv}}$ internal to $\mathcal{S}$.

The geometric theory $\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{surv}}$ itself is well-defined (Definition~\ref{def:geom-theory-surv}) and the Stone topology on $E$ is standard. The classifying property, however, has not been formally verified in \texttt{ugp-lean} or elsewhere; the argument via Diaconescu's theorem and the cylinder covers is plausible but constitutes an open problem pending formal proof. The Lawvere fixed-point theorem and Kleene recursion theorem (which are Lean-checked: \texttt{ugp\_lawvere\_fixed\_point}, \texttt{ugp\_kleene\_recursion\_theorem}) remain independent of this conjecture.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Truth values as opens]\label{prop:Omega-pred}
In $\mathbf{Sh}(E)$ the subobject classifier $\Omega$ is the sheaf of opens. For each prime $p$, the predicate ``$x\in B_p$'' corresponds to the clopen cylinder $U_p:=\pi_p^{-1}(B_p)\subset E$. Finite conjunctions and disjunctions correspond to intersections and unions of these clopens~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

% ===========================
% B3-2: Fibred level structure and Beck--Chevalley
% ===========================

% ===========================
\subsubsection{Fibred level structure and Beck--Chevalley}\label{subsec:B3-fibred}

\begin{definition}[Base category of levels]\label{def:level-base}
Let $\mathbf{Lev}$ be the category whose objects are admissible levels $n$ with ridges $R_n=2^n-16$, and whose morphisms $f:n\to m$ are \emph{finite local surgeries} of signatures: $S_p(m)=S_p(n)$ for all but finitely many primes $p$, and on the exceptional set each $S_p(m)$ is obtained from $S_p(n)$ by adding/removing finitely many residues (a clopen surgery). Write $\Sigma_n=(S_p(n))_p$ and $E(\Sigma_n)\subset \widehat{\Z}^{\times}$ for the survivor subspace at level $n$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Fibred survivor topoi]\label{prop:fibred-topoi}
The assignment $n\mapsto \mathcal{E}_n:=\mathbf{Sh}(E(\Sigma_n))$ extends to a pseudofunctor $\mathcal{E}:\mathbf{Lev}^{\mathrm{op}}\to \mathbf{Topos}$: a morphism $f:n\to m$ induces a geometric morphism $f^{\ast}:\mathcal{E}_m\to \mathcal{E}_n$ whose inverse-image is pullback along the induced continuous map $E(\Sigma_n)\to E(\Sigma_m)$ arising from the finite clopen surgery. Composition holds up to canonical isomorphism and identities act as identities~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Beck--Chevalley for finite prime squares, explicit]\label{thm:beck-chev-full}
Let $I\subset J$ be finite prime sets and $f:n\to m$ a morphism in $\mathbf{Lev}$ (finite clopen surgery of signatures). Write $E(\Sigma_n)$ and $E(\Sigma_m)$ for the survivor Stone spaces and $E_\bullet(\cdot)_K$ for their restrictions to the cylinder subspaces on coordinates $K\in\{I,J\}$.

Consider the commutative square of clopen maps of spaces
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3.6em, row sep=2.0em]
E(\Sigma_n)_J \arrow[r, hook, "i_{n}"] \arrow[d, "f_J"'] & E(\Sigma_n)_I \arrow[d, "f_I"] \\
E(\Sigma_m)_J \arrow[r, hook, "i_{m}"'] & E(\Sigma_m)_I
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where $i_{\bullet}$ are clopen embeddings forgetting coordinates $J\setminus I$, and $f_K$ is the continuous map induced by the finite surgery on coordinates in $K$ (identity elsewhere). Then for the induced geometric morphisms on sheaf topoi we have a canonical natural isomorphism
\[
i^{\ast}\, f_J^{\ast}\ \cong\ f_I^{\ast}\, i^{\ast}
\]
between the corresponding inverse-image functors $\mathbf{Sh}(E(\Sigma_m)_J)\to \mathbf{Sh}(E(\Sigma_n)_I)$. Moreover, all functors involved preserve finite limits and colimits~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{On presheaves.} Work first in presheaves on the clopen site of $E(\Sigma_\bullet)_K$. The inverse image $f_K^{\ast}$ is precomposition with $f_K$ on opens; the restriction $i^{\ast}$ is precomposition with the inclusion $i_\bullet$. Because the square of spaces commutes strictly ($i_m\circ f_J = f_I\circ i_n$), we obtain a strict equality of precomposition functors on presheaves:
\[
i^{\ast}\circ f_J^{\ast} \;=\; f_I^{\ast}\circ i^{\ast}.
\]

\textbf{Sheafification.} Let $a$ denote sheafification on the Stone sites (generated by clopens). For any presheaf $F$ on $E(\Sigma_m)_J$,
\[
i^{\ast} f_J^{\ast} (aF) \;\cong\; a\big( i^{\ast} f_J^{\ast} F \big)
\quad\text{and}\quad
f_I^{\ast} i^{\ast} (aF) \;\cong\; a\big( f_I^{\ast} i^{\ast} F \big),
\]
since both $i^{\ast}$ and $f_K^{\ast}$ preserve finite limits and arbitrary colimits on clopen sites (precomposition along continuous maps). Using the presheaf equality above and functoriality of $a$, we get a canonical natural isomorphism
\[
i^{\ast}\, f_J^{\ast} \ \cong\ f_I^{\ast}\, i^{\ast}
\]
on sheaves.

\textbf{Exactness.} Inverse-image functors of geometric morphisms are left exact and cocontinuous; on Stone sites of clopens, precomposition along clopen maps preserves finite limits and all small colimits. Hence $i^{\ast}$ and $f_K^{\ast}$ preserve both finite limits and colimits.
\end{proof}

% ===========================
% B3-3: Universal property of the product site
% ===========================

\subsubsection{Initiality under sector/branch preservation}\label{subsec:B3-universal}

\begin{theorem}[Initiality of the product site with generators and relations]\label{thm:universal-morph-full}
Let $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{CRT}}$ be the site whose objects are finite sets of congruence conditions $\{x\equiv a_j\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m_j)\}_{j=1}^k$ with $(m_j,R_n)=1$, coverings generated by refinement of congruences, and morphisms given by implication of condition-sets. Let $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{Pratt}}$ be the site whose objects are finite families of bounded-height Pratt trees (factor-branch data) with coverings generated by subtree refinement and height truncation. 

Define the \emph{product site} $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}}$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item Objects are pairs $(X,Y)$ with $X\in \mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{CRT}}$, $Y\in \mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{Pratt}}$, together with compatibility isomorphisms on generators identifying sector refinements of $X$ with branchwise restrictions of $Y$ whenever both speak about the same primes.
\item A covering family $\{(X_i,Y_i)\to (X,Y)\}_i$ is generated by either (i) a CRT refinement $X_i\to X$ with $Y_i=Y$ and inherited compatibilities, or (ii) a Pratt refinement $Y_i\to Y$, or (iii) a finite mix of (i) and (ii), provided the compatibility identifications are respected on overlaps.
\end{itemize}
Then the canonical geometric morphisms 
\[
\pi_{\mathrm{CRT}}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}})\to \mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{CRT}}),\qquad
\pi_{\mathrm{Pratt}}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}})\to \mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{Pratt}})
\]
are \emph{initial} among pairs $(F,G)$ of geometric morphisms whose inverse-image functors preserve: (i) all sector partitions modulo moduli coprime to the ridge (CRT refinement), and (ii) Pratt branch heights (height truncation). Precisely: for any topos $\mathcal{E}$ and morphisms $F:\mathcal{E}\to \mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{CRT}})$, $G:\mathcal{E}\to \mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{Pratt}})$ with $F^\ast,G^\ast$ preserving (i)–(ii), there exists a geometric morphism $H:\mathcal{E}\to \mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}})$, unique up to unique isomorphism, with $\pi_{\mathrm{CRT}}\circ H\cong F$ and $\pi_{\mathrm{Pratt}}\circ H\cong G$~\cite{SpivackUGPFormalization,ugp-lean}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{Generators and relations.} Let $\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{CRT}}$ be the full subcategory of $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{CRT}}$ on single-congruence objects $x\equiv a\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m)$; let $\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{Pratt}}$ be the full subcategory of $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{Pratt}}$ on single-branch, single-height generators. Every object is a finite limit/colimit of generators, and coverings are generated by the evident epimorphic families (CRT refinements; branch truncations).

Form the 2-pushout in the 2-category of sites by freely adjoining to the disjoint union category $\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{CRT}}\sqcup \mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{Pratt}}$ the relations that identify shared prime coordinates: whenever a generator $x\equiv a\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)$ mentions a prime $p$ also present in a branch-generator, we add an isomorphism between the corresponding sector and branch restriction functors. The resulting site (completed under finite limits and the generated cover system) is precisely $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}}$.

\textbf{Constructing $H$.} Given $F$ and $G$ as in the statement, the inverse images $F^\ast$ and $G^\ast$ assign in $\mathcal{E}$ objects to each generator, respecting CRT refinements and branch truncations. Because (i)–(ii) hold, these assignments agree on the imposed relations (the shared prime coordinates). Thus there is a unique (up to unique isomorphism) functor on generators $\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{CRT}}\sqcup \mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{Pratt}}\to \mathcal{E}$ that coequalizes the relations; by the universal property of the 2-pushout of sites, this extends uniquely to a morphism of sites $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}}\to \mathcal{E}$. Passing to sheaves, we obtain the desired geometric morphism $H:\mathcal{E}\to \mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}})$.

\textbf{Uniqueness.} Any other $H'$ with $\pi_{\mathrm{CRT}}\circ H'\cong F$ and $\pi_{\mathrm{Pratt}}\circ H'\cong G$ induces the same assignments on generators and respects the same relations; by freeness, $H'\cong H$ uniquely.

\textbf{Exactness.} Inverse images along $\pi_{\mathrm{CRT}}$ and $\pi_{\mathrm{Pratt}}$ are geometric (left exact, cocontinuous). Because coverings in $\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{prod}}$ are generated by coverings in the components and the added relations are identities on overlaps, exactness follows by stability of finite limits/colimits under pullback along clopen embeddings (our cylinder sites are Stone).
\end{proof}
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\FitToWidth{%
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=0.7\textwidth,

    grid=major,
]

  coordinates {
    (16,86) (18,81) (21,76) (24,73) (28,71) (36,71) (42,73) (56,81) (63,86)
  };
\addlegendentry{$b_1(b_2)$}
% Plot AM-GM bound
\addplot+[domain=16:63, samples=50, dashed, color=red] {2*sqrt(1008)+7};
\addlegendentry{$2\sqrt{R}+7$}
% Highlight 24,42
